Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Understand the User Request:**
   - **Function:** `def stable_toposort(graph):`
   - **Input:** `graph` - a dictionary mapping a node to its dependencies (list/set/iterable of nodes it depends on).
   - **Output:** A list of nodes in dependency-first order (topological order).
   - **Stability Requirement:** When multiple nodes are ready (i.e., all dependencies satisfied), they should be returned in the order they were originally inserted into the dictionary.
   - **Error Handling:** Raise `ValueError` for missing dependencies or cycles.
   - **Constraint:** Do not mutate the input.
   - **Output Format:** Python code only.

2.  **Key Concepts & Challenges:**
   - Topological sort using Kahn's algorithm (in-degree based) is natural here.
   - Stability requirement: When multiple nodes have in-degree 0, we need to pick them in the order they appear in the original dictionary. This suggests using a queue or priority queue that respects insertion order. Python's `collections.deque` or a simple list/queue can work, but we need to efficiently get the "next" ready node in insertion order. Actually, we can just maintain a queue of ready nodes, and when we process a node, we add newly ready nodes to the queue. To maintain insertion order among ready nodes, we should add them to the queue in the order we encounter them, but we also need to respect the original dict order for initial ready nodes.
   - Wait, Kahn's algorithm typically uses a queue. If we initialize the queue with nodes that have in-degree 0 in the order they appear in `graph.keys()`, and when we process a node, we decrement in-degrees of its dependents, and when a dependent's in-degree hits 0, we add it to the queue. But the order of adding to the queue matters for stability.
   - Actually, the stability requirement says: "using original dict insertion order whenever multiple nodes are ready." This means if nodes A, B, C are all ready at the same time, they should be processed in the order A, B, C as they appeared in the dict.
   - Kahn's algorithm with a queue naturally processes nodes in FIFO order. If we initialize the queue with initial zero-in-degree nodes in dict insertion order, and when we add newly ready nodes, we need to ensure they are added in a way that preserves the relative order among all ready nodes. But wait, if we just append to the queue, the order will be: initial ready nodes (in dict order), then newly ready nodes as they are discovered. But newly discovered nodes might have been earlier in the dict than some already-queued nodes. Does the requirement mean we should always pick the earliest-in-dict among currently ready nodes? Yes, "using original dict insertion order whenever multiple nodes are ready."
   - To strictly satisfy this, we might need a priority queue or just a sorted structure, but that's O(N log N). Alternatively, we can use a simple list/queue and sort/filter, but that's inefficient.
   - Actually, there's a simpler way: maintain a set of ready nodes, and always pick the one that appears earliest in the original dict order among the ready set. We can precompute the insertion order index for each node. Then use a min-heap or just iterate? But iterating over a set to find the min index each time is O(N^2).
   - Wait, Kahn's algorithm with a queue actually works if we are careful. Let's think: If we use a queue and initialize it with zero-in-degree nodes in dict order, when we process a node u, we decrement in-degree of v. If v becomes ready, we add it to the queue. But v might have appeared earlier in the dict than some nodes already in the queue. Does the requirement require strict dict order among *all* currently ready nodes, or just that the algorithm is deterministic and stable? "using original dict insertion order whenever multiple nodes are ready" strongly implies that if {A, B} are ready, and A appeared before B in the dict, A must be processed first.
   - To guarantee this, we can use a priority queue with the insertion order index as the key. Python's `heapq` works. We'll assign each node an index based on `enumerate(graph.keys())`. Then use a min-heap of `(index, node)`. This ensures that when multiple nodes are ready, the one with the smallest index (earliest in dict) is popped first. This is O(N log N) or O(N log D) where D is max degree, which is fine.
   - Alternatively, we can use a simple queue but process nodes in batches? No.
   - Let's stick with the heap approach for strict stability. It's clean and efficient enough.

3.  **Algorithm Design (Heap-based Kahn's):**
   - Compute in-degrees for all nodes. Note: dependencies might reference nodes not in the graph? The problem says "graph maps a node to its dependencies". Usually, all nodes are keys in the dict. But dependencies might include nodes not in keys? The problem says "Raise ValueError for a missing dependency". This likely means if a dependency is not in the graph keys, raise ValueError. Or if a node depends on something not defined. I'll check if all dependencies exist in the graph keys. If not, raise ValueError.
   - Actually, "missing dependency" could also mean a dependency node is not in the graph at all. I'll validate that all dependencies are keys in the graph.
   - Steps:
     1. Validate that all dependencies exist in `graph.keys()`. If not, raise ValueError.
     2. Compute in-degrees: for each node, count how many nodes depend on it. Actually, in-degree = number of dependencies? Wait, in topological sort, in-degree usually means number of incoming edges. If graph maps node -> dependencies, then an edge goes from dependency -> node. So in-degree of a node = len(graph[node]).
     3. Assign insertion order indices: `order = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(graph.keys())}`
     4. Initialize a min-heap with `(order[node], node)` for all nodes with in-degree 0.
     5. While heap is not empty:
        - Pop `(idx, u)`
        - Append `u` to result
        - For each node `v` that depends on `u`? Wait, we need reverse adjacency list to efficiently find dependents. Or we can just iterate over all nodes and check dependencies? That's O(N^2). Better to build a reverse graph (dependents map).
        - Actually, we can build `dependents = {node: [] for node in graph}`. For each `u` in `graph`, for each `dep` in `graph[u]`, add `u` to `dependents[dep]`.
        - Then when processing `u`, for each `v` in `dependents[u]`, decrement in-degree. If in-degree becomes 0, push `(order[v], v)` to heap.
     6. If `len(result) < len(graph)`, raise ValueError (cycle detected).
     7. Return result.

   - Wait, what about "missing dependency"? If `graph[u]` contains a node not in `graph.keys()`, that's a missing dependency. I should check that during reverse graph construction or validation.
   - Let's refine validation: For each `u` in `graph`, for each `dep` in `graph[u]`, if `dep` not in `graph`, raise ValueError.
   - Also, what if a node has a dependency on itself? That's a cycle, caught by the result length check.
   - The heap approach ensures stability. Let's verify: If multiple nodes become ready, they are pushed to the heap with their insertion indices. The heap pops the smallest index first, which matches dict insertion order. This satisfies the requirement.

4.  **Edge Cases & Details:**
   - Empty graph: returns `[]`
   - Graph with no dependencies: returns keys in insertion order
   - Cycle: caught by `len(result) != len(graph)`
   - Missing dependency: caught by validation
   - Do not mutate input: I'm only reading from `graph`, building new structures. Good.
   - What if dependencies are given as sets or other iterables? I'll convert to list or just iterate.
   - The problem says "graph maps a node to its dependencies". I'll assume `graph` is a dict.
   - I need to be careful with the reverse graph construction. `dependents` should map a dependency to a list of nodes that depend on it.
   - Let's draft the code:

```python
import heapq

def stable_toposort(graph):
    # Validate dependencies and build reverse graph
    dependents = {node: [] for node in graph}
    in_degree = {node: 0 for node in graph}

    for node, deps in graph.items():
        for dep in deps:
            if dep not in graph:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep}")
            dependents[dep].append(node)
            in_degree[node] += 1

    # Insertion order indices for stability
    order = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(graph.keys())}

    # Initialize heap with nodes that have no dependencies
    heap = [(order[node], node) for node in graph if in_degree[node] == 0]
    heapq.heapify(heap)

    result = []
    while heap:
        _, u = heapq.heappop(heap)
        result.append(u)
        for v in dependents[u]:
            in_degree[v] -= 1
            if in_degree[v] == 0:
                heapq.heappush(heap, (order[v], v))

    if len(result) != len(graph):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected in graph")

    return result
```

5.  **Check Stability Requirement:**
   - "using original dict insertion order whenever multiple nodes are ready"
   - My heap approach uses `(order[v], v)` as the key. Since `order` is based on insertion order, when multiple nodes are in the heap, the one with the smallest `order` value (earliest insertion) is popped first. This strictly satisfies the requirement.
   - Wait, is there a more efficient way without a heap? If we just use a queue and process nodes in batches by insertion order, it might work, but heap is simpler and O(N log N) is perfectly acceptable for topological sort. Actually, Kahn's algorithm with a queue is O(N+M), but to maintain strict insertion order among ready nodes, we'd need to sort or use a priority queue. Given Python's `heapq`, it's fine.
   - Could there be a case where `order` values are not needed if we just use a queue and initialize it properly? Suppose we have nodes A, B, C in dict order. Dependencies: A->[], B->[], C->[A]. Initially ready: A, B. Queue: [A, B]. Pop A, process. C becomes ready. Queue: [B, C]. Pop B, then C. Order: A, B, C. Matches dict order.
   - What if dict order is C, A, B. Dependencies: A->[], B->[], C->[A]. Initially ready: A, B. But in dict order, C is first, but C isn't ready. So ready set is {A, B}. Dict order among ready: A, B. Queue: [A, B]. Pop A -> C ready. Queue: [B, C]. Pop B, then C. Order: A, B, C. Dict order: C, A, B. Among ready nodes, A comes before B. So A, B, C is correct.
   - Actually, a simple queue works if we initialize it with initial zero-in-degree nodes in dict order, and when we add newly ready nodes, we add them to the end. But does this guarantee that among *all* currently ready nodes, the earliest in dict order is picked? Not necessarily. Example: Dict order: A, B, C. Dependencies: A->[], B->[A], C->[A]. Initially ready: A. Queue: [A]. Pop A. B and C become ready. We add B then C to queue. Queue: [B, C]. Pop B, then C. Order: A, B, C. Dict order: A, B, C. Matches.
   - Counterexample for simple queue: Dict order: A, B, C. Dependencies: A->[], B->[], C->[A]. Initially ready: A, B. Queue: [A, B]. Pop A. C becomes ready. Queue: [B, C]. Pop B, then C. Order: A, B, C. Dict order: A, B, C. Matches.
   - What if Dict order: B, A, C. Dependencies: A->[], B->[], C->[A]. Initially ready: B, A. But in dict order, B comes before A. So ready set in dict order: B, A. Queue should be [B, A]. Pop B. Queue: [A]. Pop A. C becomes ready. Queue: [C]. Order: B, A, C. Dict order: B, A, C. Matches.
   - It seems a simple queue initialized with initial ready nodes in dict order, and appending newly ready nodes to the end, actually preserves the relative order correctly! Why? Because newly ready nodes are discovered when processing predecessors. Their discovery time is after the predecessors. But does it guarantee that if X and Y are both ready, and X appeared before Y in the dict, X is processed first?
   - Let's test a tricky case: Dict order: A, B, C, D. Dependencies: A->[], B->[], C->[A], D->[B]. Initially ready: A, B. Queue: [A, B]. Pop A -> C ready. Queue: [B, C]. Pop B -> D ready. Queue: [C, D]. Pop C, D. Order: A, B, C, D. Dict order: A, B, C, D. Matches.
   - Another: Dict order: C, A, B. Dependencies: A->[], B->[], C->[A]. Initially ready: A, B. In dict order among ready: A, B. Queue: [A, B]. Pop A -> C ready. Queue: [B, C]. Pop B, C. Order: A, B, C. Dict order: C, A, B. Among ready, A before B. So A, B, C is correct.
   - Actually, the simple queue approach works because topological sort processes nodes level by level (in terms of dependency depth), and within each "wave", the order is determined by when they become ready. But the requirement says "using original dict insertion order whenever multiple nodes are ready." This implies a strict priority on insertion order over discovery time.
   - Let's construct a case where simple queue fails: Dict order: A, B, C. Dependencies: A->[], B->[], C->[A]. Initially ready: A, B. Queue: [A, B]. Pop A. C becomes ready. Queue: [B, C]. Now B and C are ready. Dict order: B comes before C. So B should be popped next. Queue gives B. Correct.
   - What if Dict order: C, A, B. Dependencies: A->[], B->[], C->[A]. Initially ready: A, B. Dict order among ready: A, B. Queue: [A, B]. Pop A -> C ready. Queue: [B, C]. Now B and C ready. Dict order: B before C. Queue gives B. Correct.
   - It seems the simple queue actually works for this stability requirement! Why? Because if X and Y are both ready, and X appeared before Y in the dict, then either:
     1. Both were initially ready -> added to queue in dict order -> X before Y in queue.
     2. X was initially ready, Y became ready later -> X is already in queue, Y is appended -> X before Y.
     3. Y was initially ready, X became ready later -> But if X became ready later, it means X depends on some node Z. But Y is already ready. If X appeared before Y in dict, but Y was ready earlier, then Y would be in queue before X is added. So Y would be processed before X. This violates the requirement!

```python
def stable_toposort(graph):
    """
    Perform a stable topological sort on the given graph.

    Args:
        graph: A dict mapping each node to its list of dependencies.

    Returns:
        A list of nodes in dependency-first order. When multiple nodes are ready,
        they are ordered by their original insertion order in the graph dict.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If a dependency is not present in the graph or if there's a cycle.
    """
    # Make a copy of the graph to avoid mutating input
    # We need to track remaining dependencies for each node
    # and also track which nodes depend on each node (reverse adjacency)

    # Get all nodes in original insertion order
    nodes_in_order = list(graph.keys())
    node_set = set(nodes_in_order)

    # Check for missing dependencies
    for node, deps in graph.items():
        for dep in deps:
            if dep not in node_set:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep} not found in graph")

    # Build reverse adjacency: for each node, which nodes depend on it
    dependents = {node: [] for node in nodes_in_order}
    # Track remaining dependency count for each node
    remaining_deps = {}

    for node, deps in graph.items():
        remaining_deps[node] = len(deps)
        for dep in deps:
            dependents[dep].append(node)

    # Initialize queue with nodes that have no dependencies, in original insertion order
    from collections import deque

    # Use a list as a priority-like structure based on original insertion order
    # We'll use a simple approach: maintain a list of ready nodes, and pick from them
    # in the order they appear in the original graph

    # Map node to its index in the original order for stable sorting
    node_index = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(nodes_in_order)}

    # Start with all nodes having 0 dependencies, sorted by original insertion order
    ready = [node for node in nodes_in_order if remaining_deps[node] == 0]
    # ready is already in original insertion order since we iterate nodes_in_order

    result = []

    while ready:
        # Pick the first ready node (which is in original insertion order among ready nodes)
        # But we need to ensure that when new nodes become ready, they are inserted
        # in the correct position relative to other ready nodes

        # Actually, let me think more carefully. The requirement says "using original
        # dict insertion order whenever multiple nodes are ready". This means at each step,
        # among all currently ready nodes, we should pick the one that appears first
        # in the original dict insertion order.

        # So we should always pick the ready node with the smallest original index.
        # Let's use a min-heap or just find the min each time. For simplicity, let's
        # keep ready as a list and always pick the one with smallest node_index.

        # Find the ready node with the smallest original insertion index
        min_idx = float('inf')
        chosen = None
        for node in ready:
            idx = node_index[node]
            if idx < min_idx:
                min_idx = idx
                chosen = node

        result.append(chosen)
        ready.remove(chosen)

        # Now, for each node that depends on chosen, decrement its remaining deps
        for dependent in dependents[chosen]:
            remaining_deps[dependent] -= 1
            if remaining_deps[dependent] == 0:
                ready.append(dependent)

    if len(result) != len(nodes_in_order):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected in graph")

    return result
```

```python
import heapq


def stable_toposort(graph):
    in_degree = {}
    dependents = {node: [] for node in graph}

    for node, deps in graph.items():
        unique_deps = set(deps)
        in_degree[node] = len(unique_deps)
        for dep in unique_deps:
            if dep not in graph:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep!r}")
            dependents[dep].append(node)

    order_index = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(graph)}

    heap = []
    for node in graph:
        if in_degree[node] == 0:
            heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[node], node))

    result = []
    while heap:
        _, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        result.append(node)
        for dependent in dependents[node]:
            in_degree[dependent] -= 1
            if in_degree[dependent] == 0:
                heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[dependent], dependent))

    if len(result) != len(graph):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected")

    return result
```
reasoning
Let me think through this carefully.

We have a `graph` that maps a node to its dependencies. So `graph = {A: [B, C], B: [], C: []}` means A depends on B and C.

We need to return a "dependency-first order" - meaning if A depends on B, B comes before A in the result.

Constraints:
1. Use original dict insertion order whenever multiple nodes are ready (i.e., when multiple nodes have all their dependencies satisfied, pick the one that appears first in the original dict's insertion order).
2. Raise ValueError for a missing dependency (a dependency that isn't a key in the graph) or a cycle.
3. Do not mutate the input.

Let me think about the algorithm:

1. First, validate that all dependencies exist in the graph (are keys). If any dependency is not a key in the graph, raise ValueError.
2. Use Kahn's algorithm (topological sort) but with a priority based on the original insertion order.

For Kahn's algorithm:
- Compute in-degree for each node (number of dependencies it has, or rather, number of nodes that depend on it... wait, let me think again).

Actually, let me reconsider. The graph maps a node to its dependencies. So if A depends on B, then B must come before A.

In terms of topological sort:
- Edge from B to A (B must come before A)
- In-degree of A is the number of dependencies A has (i.e., len(graph[A]))
- A node is "ready" when its in-degree is 0 (all dependencies are satisfied)

Wait, let me reconsider the direction. If A depends on B, then B must appear before A. So the edge is B → A. The in-degree of a node is the number of its dependencies (edges pointing to it from its dependencies).

So:
- in_degree[node] = len(graph[node])
- Initially, nodes with in_degree 0 are ready.
- When we process a node, we need to find all nodes that depend on it and decrement their in_degree.

To find "all nodes that depend on it", I need a reverse adjacency list: for each node, which nodes list it as a dependency.

Let me think about the "original dict insertion order" constraint. When multiple nodes are ready, we pick the one that appears first in the original dict's insertion order.

So I need to maintain a priority based on the original insertion order. I can assign each node an index based on its position in the dict's keys, and always pick the ready node with the smallest index.

I can use a sorted structure or simply scan through the ready set each time picking the one with the smallest original index. For efficiency, I could use a heap with the original index as priority.

```python
import heapq

def stable_toposort(graph):
    # Build reverse adjacency and in-degrees
    # in_degree[node] = number of dependencies (edges from deps to node)
    in_degree = {node: len(deps) for node, deps in graph.items()}

    # Reverse adjacency: for each dependency, which nodes depend on it
    dependents = {node: [] for node in graph}
    for node, deps in graph.items():
        for dep in deps:
            if dep not in graph:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep}")
            dependents[dep].append(node)

    # Original insertion order index
    order_index = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(graph)}

    # Initialize heap with nodes that have in_degree 0
    heap = []
    for node in graph:
        if in_degree[node] == 0:
            heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[node], node))

    result = []
    while heap:
        _, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        result.append(node)
        for dependent in dependents[node]:
            in_degree[dependent] -= 1
            if in_degree[dependent] == 0:
                heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[dependent], dependent))

    if len(result) != len(graph):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected")

    return result
```

Wait, I need to be careful about not mutating the input. I'm creating new dicts (`in_degree`, `dependents`, `order_index`) and a new list (`result`), so the input `graph` is not mutated. The `deps` lists are iterated but not modified. Good.

Let me also consider: what if a node has duplicate dependencies? Like `graph = {A: [B, B]}`. The in_degree would be 2, but when we process B, we'd decrement A's in_degree by 1 (since B appears once in dependents[B]). That would be a bug.

Hmm, let me think about this. If `graph = {A: [B, B]}`, then A depends on B (listed twice). The in_degree should probably be 1 (A has 1 unique dependency), or should it be 2?

```python
import heapq

def stable_toposort(graph):
    in_degree = {}
    dependents = {node: [] for node in graph}

    for node, deps in graph.items():
        unique_deps = set(deps)
        in_degree[node] = len(unique_deps)
        for dep in unique_deps:
            if dep not in graph:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep}")
            dependents[dep].append(node)

    order_index = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(graph)}

    heap = []
    for node in graph:
        if in_degree[node] == 0:
            heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[node], node))

    result = []
    while heap:
        _, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        result.append(node)
        for dependent in dependents[node]:
            in_degree[dependent] -= 1
            if in_degree[dependent] == 0:
                heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[dependent], dependent))

    if len(result) != len(graph):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected")

    return result
```

This looks good. Let me trace through an example:

`graph = {'A': ['B', 'C'], 'B': [], 'C': []}`

- in_degree: A=2, B=0, C=0
- dependents: A=[], B=['A'], C=['A']
- order_index: A=0, B=1, C=2
- Initial heap: (1, 'B'), (2, 'C')
- Pop B → result = ['B'], decrement A's in_degree to 1
- Pop C → result = ['B', 'C'], decrement A's in_degree to 0, push (0, 'A')
- Pop A → result = ['B', 'C', 'A']

- Pop C → result = ['A', 'B', 'C']

```python
import heapq

def stable_toposort(graph):
    """
    Perform a stable topological sort of a dependency graph.

    Parameters:
        graph (dict): Mapping from node to an iterable of its dependencies.

    Returns:
        list: Nodes in dependency-first order, stable with respect to
              the original insertion order of the keys.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If a dependency is missing or a cycle exists.
    """
    if not graph:
        return []

    # Assign an index to each node based on insertion order.
    node_index = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(graph)}

    # Build indegree and dependents.
    indegree = {node: 0 for node in graph}
    dependents = {node: [] for node in graph}

    for node, deps in graph.items():
        for dep in deps:
            if dep not in graph:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep} required by {node}")
            indegree[node] += 1
            dependents[dep].append(node)

    # Initialize ready nodes (indegree 0) using a min-heap by index.
    ready_heap = []
    for node, deg in indegree.items():
        if deg == 0:
            heapq.heappush(ready_heap, (node_index[node], node))

    result = []
    while ready_heap:
        _, node = heapq.heappop(ready_heap)
        result.append(node)
        for dependent in dependents[node]:
            indegree[dependent] -= 1
            if indegree[dependent] == 0:
                heapq.heappush(ready_heap, (node_index[dependent], dependent))

    if len(result) != len(graph):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected in the dependency graph")

    return result
```
reasoning
We need to implement stable_toposort(graph). The graph is a dict mapping node to its dependencies (list or iterable). We need to return a list of nodes in dependency-first order. Use original dict insertion order whenever multiple nodes are ready. That means we need to maintain the order of nodes as they appear in the dict keys. When multiple nodes become ready (i.e., all dependencies satisfied), we should pick them in the order they appear in the original dict insertion order. Also handle missing dependency (node not in graph) and cycles.

We must not mutate input.

We need to return Python code only. So we'll write a function definition.

Approach: We'll use Kahn's algorithm with a priority based on insertion order. Since we need original dict insertion order, we can assign an index to each node based on its position in graph keys. Then when we have a set of nodes with zero indegree (ready), we choose the one with smallest index.

We need to track dependencies: each node has dependencies; we need to compute reverse dependencies (dependents) for decrementing.

We'll also detect missing dependency: if a node's dependency is not in graph, raise ValueError.

Cycle detection: if we cannot process all nodes, raise ValueError.

Implementation steps:

1. Validate input: graph is dict, keys are hashable, values are iterable of dependencies.
2. Create a mapping from node to index (position in graph keys) for tie-breaking.
3. Build indegree: number of dependencies for each node. But careful: dependencies might be repeated? Usually not, but we can handle by counting unique dependencies? Probably not needed. We'll count each dependency occurrence, but if duplicates, indegree will be >1 and we need to decrement for each occurrence. That's fine.
4. Build dependents: dict mapping dependency -> list of nodes that depend on it.
5. For each node, for each dep in graph[node]: if dep not in graph: raise ValueError. Also add node to dependents[dep].
6. Initialize ready queue: nodes with indegree 0. We'll use a min-heap based on index, but we need to pop in order of index. Since we need to pick the smallest index among ready nodes, we can use a heap with (index, node). But we also need to add new ready nodes as we process.
7. Process: while heap not empty, pop smallest index node, add to result, for each dependent, decrement indegree, if becomes 0, push to heap.
8. After loop, if len(result) != len(graph): cycle -> raise ValueError.

Edge cases: empty graph -> return [].

We must not mutate input, so we create copies.

```python
def schema_drift(expected, actual):
    """
    Compare two nested dicts with type string leaves.
    Return sorted list of dotted paths for:
    - Missing fields (in expected but not in actual)
    - Extra fields (in actual but not in expected)
    - Type mismatches (type differs between expected and actual)

    For missing/extra nested objects, report their leaf paths.
    """
    results = []

    def _compare(exp, act, prefix=""):
        if exp is None or act is None:
            # If either is None at this point, we might have a mismatch
            # But the problem states leaves are type strings, so we expect both to be dicts until leaves
            return

        # Get all keys from both dicts
        all_keys = set(list(exp.keys()) + list(act.keys()))

        for key in all_keys:
            dotted_key = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key

            if key not in exp:
                # Key is extra in actual
                # Report leaf paths under this key in actual
                _report_leaf_paths(act[key], dotted_key, "extra")
            elif key not in act:
                # Key is missing in actual
                # Report leaf paths under this key in expected
                _report_leaf_paths(exp[key], dotted_key, "missing")
            else:
                # Key exists in both
                exp_val = exp[key]
                act_val = act[key]

                # Check if both are dicts (nested structure)
                if isinstance(exp_val, dict) and isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    _compare(exp_val, act_val, dotted_key)
                elif isinstance(exp_val, str) and isinstance(act_val, str):
                    # Both are leaves (type strings)
                    if exp_val != act_val:
                        results.append(dotted_key)
                else:
                    # One is a dict, the other is a leaf (or vice versa)
                    # This is a structural mismatch; report leaf paths from both sides
                    # Treat as both missing and extra? Let's think...
                    # If expected has a dict here but actual has a string,
                    # then the expected leaves are missing and actual leaf is extra.
                    if isinstance(exp_val, dict):
                        _report_leaf_paths(exp_val, dotted_key, "missing")
                        results.append(dotted_key)  # The actual value itself is extra
                    elif isinstance(act_val, dict):
                        _report_leaf_paths(act_val, dotted_key, "extra")
                        results.append(dotted_key)  # The expected value itself is missing
                    else:
                        # Both are non-dict, non-string? Shouldn't happen per spec
                        results.append(dotted_key)

    def _report_leaf_paths(node, path_prefix, kind):
        """Report all leaf paths under node with the given kind."""
        if isinstance(node, dict):
            for key, value in node.items():
                child_path = f"{path_prefix}.{key}"
                _report_leaf_paths(value, child_path, kind)
        else:
            # It's a leaf (type string)
            results.append((path_prefix, kind))

    # Wait, the above approach mixes things up. Let me reconsider.
    # The function should return sorted dotted paths. Each path indicates an issue.
    # For missing fields: paths that exist in expected but not in actual (including all sub-paths if it's a nested object)
    # For extra fields: paths that exist in actual but not in expected (including all sub-paths if it's a nested object)
    # For type differences: paths where both exist but types differ

    # Let me redo this more carefully.
    results = []

    def _collect_leaves(d, prefix):
        """Collect all leaf paths from a dict."""
        leaves = []
        for key, value in d.items():
            p = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            if isinstance(value, dict):
                leaves.extend(_collect_leaves(value, p))
            else:
                leaves.append(p)
        return leaves

    def _compare_recursive(exp, act, prefix=""):
        """Recursively compare expected and actual dicts."""
        if not isinstance(exp, dict) or not isinstance(act, dict):
            return

        exp_keys = set(exp.keys())
        act_keys = set(act.keys())

        # Keys only in expected (missing in actual)
        missing_keys = exp_keys - act_keys
        # Keys only in actual (extra in actual)
        extra_keys = act_keys - exp_keys
        # Keys in both
        common_keys = exp_keys & act_keys

        # Handle missing keys
        for key in missing_keys:
            p = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            # Collect all leaf paths under this missing key in expected
            leaves = _collect_leaves(exp[key], p)
            results.extend(leaves)

        # Handle extra keys
        for key in extra_keys:
            p = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else p
            p = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            # Collect all leaf paths under this extra key in actual
            leaves = _collect_leaves(act[key], p)
            results.extend(leaves)

        # Handle common keys
        for key in common_keys:
            p = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            exp_val = exp[key]
            act_val = act[key]

            if isinstance(exp_val, dict) and isinstance(act_val, dict):
                _compare_recursive(exp_val, act_val, p)
            elif isinstance(exp_val, str) and isinstance(act_val, str):
                if exp_val != act_val:
                    results.append(p)
            else:
                # Structural mismatch: one is dict, other is leaf, or both are different types
                # Report all leaf paths from expected as missing, and all leaf paths from actual as extra
                if isinstance(exp_val, dict):
                    leaves_exp = _collect_leaves(exp_val, p)
                    results.extend(leaves_exp)
                else:
                    results.append(p)  # The leaf itself is considered missing structure

                if isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    leaves_act = _collect_leaves(act_val, p)
                    results.extend(leaves_act)
                else:
                    results.append(p)  # The leaf itself is considered extra structure

    _compare_recursive(expected, actual)

    # Sort and remove duplicates
    unique_results = sorted(set(results))

    return unique_results
```

```python
def schema_drift(expected, actual):
    def get_leaves(d, path):
        res = []
        if isinstance(d, dict):
            for k, v in d.items():
                res.extend(get_leaves(v, path + [k]))
        else:
            res.append(".".join(path))
        return res

    def compare(exp, act, path):
        res = []
        # Union of keys to catch missing and extra
        keys = set(list(exp.keys()) + list(act.keys()))

        for k in keys:
            curr_path = path + [k]
            in_exp = k in exp
            in_act = k in act

            if in_exp and in_act:
                e_val = exp[k]
                a_val = act[k]

                if isinstance(e_val, dict) and isinstance(a_val, dict):
                    res.extend(compare(e_val, a_val, curr_path))
                else:
                    # Type mismatch or structure mismatch
                    # If one is dict and other is string, or strings differ
                    if e_val != a_val:
                        # Check if it's a type string mismatch
                        # The prompt implies leaves are type strings.
                        # If we are here, at least one is not a dict (or both are dicts but handled above)
                        # Actually, if both are dicts, we recursed.
                        # So here, at least one is a leaf (string).
                        # If both are strings and differ -> mismatch
                        # If one is string, one is dict -> mismatch
                        # But wait, if e_val is dict and a_val is string, e_val != a_val is True.
                        # But strictly speaking, if e_val is dict, it's not a leaf.
                        # The prompt says "leaves are type strings".
                        # So if we are at a node where one is dict and one is string, it's a structural drift.
                        # Should we report the path or expand?
                        # "A missing/extra nested object reports its leaf paths."
                        # This specific instruction applies to missing/extra keys.
                        # For type mismatch, usually we report the key.
                        # Let's assume reporting the key `curr_path` is correct for mismatches.
                        res.append(".".join(curr_path))

            elif in_exp and not in_act:
                # Missing in actual
                e_val = exp[k]
                if isinstance(e_val, dict):
                    # Report leaf paths of the missing nested object
                    res.extend(get_leaves(e_val, curr_path))
                else:
                    # Missing leaf
                    res.append(".".join(curr_path))

            elif not in_exp and in_act:
                # Extra in actual
                a_val = act[k]
                if isinstance(a_val, dict):
                    # Report leaf paths of the extra nested object
                    res.extend(get_leaves(a_val, curr_path))
                else:
                    # Extra leaf
                    res.append(".".join(curr_path))

        return res

    results = compare(expected, actual, [])
    return sorted(results)
```

```python
def schema_drift(expected, actual):
    """
    Compare two nested dicts representing schemas.
    Leaves are type strings.
    Returns a sorted list of dotted paths where:
      - A field is missing in 'actual' (present in 'expected')
      - A field is extra in 'actual' (not present in 'expected')
      - A leaf field has a different type

    For missing/extra nested objects, report all their leaf paths.
    """
    drifts = []

    def _compare(exp, act, prefix):
        # exp and act should both be dicts at this level
        if not isinstance(exp, dict) or not isinstance(act, dict):
            # Both should be leaves (type strings) at this point
            # If one is dict and other is string, that's a type mismatch
            # But per problem, leaves are type strings, so we handle at parent level
            return

        exp_keys = set(exp.keys())
        act_keys = set(act.keys())

        # Missing fields: in expected but not in actual
        for key in exp_keys - act_keys:
            path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            _collect_leaf_paths(exp[key], path, drifts)

        # Extra fields: in actual but not in expected
        for key in act_keys - exp_keys:
            path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            _collect_leaf_paths(act[key], path, drifts)

        # Common keys: compare
        for key in exp_keys & act_keys:
            path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            exp_val = exp[key]
            act_val = act[key]

            if isinstance(exp_val, dict) and isinstance(act_val, dict):
                _compare(exp_val, act_val, path)
            elif isinstance(exp_val, str) and isinstance(act_val, str):
                if exp_val != act_val:
                    drifts.append(path)
            else:
                # Type mismatch: one is dict, other is string
                # Report the leaf paths
                if isinstance(exp_val, dict):
                    _collect_leaf_paths(exp_val, path, drifts)
                if isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    _collect_leaf_paths(act_val, path, drifts)
                # If one is dict and other is string, we've collected from the dict side
                # The string side doesn't have sub-paths, so just note it's a drift
                # Actually, if one is dict and other is string, it's a structural mismatch.
                # We should report the leaf paths of the dict side.
                # But what about the string side? It's a single leaf.
                # Let's think: if expected has a nested object and actual has a string,
                # then all leaf paths under expected's object are "missing" in actual,
                # and actual's string is an "extra" leaf at that path?
                # Hmm, this is tricky. Let me re-read the problem.

                # "A missing/extra nested object reports its leaf paths."
                # So if expected has a nested object at some path and actual doesn't have it (or has something else),
                # we report the leaf paths of the nested object as missing.
                # Similarly for extra.

                # In this case, since types differ (dict vs string), we treat it as:
                # - All leaves under the dict are missing (if dict is in expected) or extra (if dict is in actual)
                # - The string itself... well, if expected is string and actual is dict, then expected's string is missing? No.

                # Let me simplify: if they're both strings, compare directly.
                # If one is dict and other is string, collect leaf paths from the dict side.
                # This handles the case where structure changed.
                pass

    def _collect_leaf_paths(value, path, out):
        """Collect all leaf paths under value, prefixed with path."""
        if isinstance(value, dict):
            if not value:
                # Empty dict - no leaves
                return
            for k, v in value.items():
                sub_path = f"{path}.{k}" if path else k
                _collect_leaf_paths(v, sub_path, out)
        else:
            # It's a leaf (string)
            out.append(path)

    _compare(expected, actual, "")
    return sorted(drifts)
```

```python
def schema_drift(expected, actual):
    results = set()

    def _collect_leaves(d, prefix):
        for k, v in d.items():
            path = f"{prefix}.{k}" if prefix else k
            if isinstance(v, dict):
                _collect_leaves(v, path)
            else:
                results.add(path)
```

```python
def schema_drift(expected, actual):
    results = set()

    def _collect_leaves(d, prefix):
        """Collect all leaf paths in a dict."""
        for k, v in d.items():
            path = f"{prefix}.{k}" if prefix else k
            if isinstance(v, dict):
                _collect_leaves(v, path)
            else:
                results.add(path)

    def _compare(exp, act, prefix):
        for key in exp:
            path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            if key not in act:
                # Missing field
                if isinstance(exp[key], dict):
                    _collect_leaves(exp[key], path)
                else:
                    results.add(path)
            else:
                exp_val = exp[key]
                act_val = act[key]
                if isinstance(exp_val, dict) and isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    _compare(exp_val, act_val, path)
                elif isinstance(exp_val, dict) or isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    # One is nested, other is leaf - type mismatch
                    results.add(path)
                else:
                    # Both are leaves
                    if exp_val != act_val:
                        results.add(path)

        for key in act:
            if key not in exp:
                path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
                # Extra field
                if isinstance(act[key], dict):
                    _collect_leaves(act[key], path)
                else:
                    results.add(path)

    _compare(expected, actual, "")
    return sorted(results)
```

```python
def schema_drift(expected, actual):
    """
    Compare two nested dict schemas and return sorted dotted paths for
    missing fields, extra fields, or fields whose type differs.
    """
    missing = set()
    extra = set()
    mismatch = set()

    def get_leaves(d, prefix):
        """Yield all leaf paths under dict d, prefixed by prefix."""
        for k, v in d.items():
            new_prefix = f"{prefix}.{k}" if prefix else k
            if isinstance(v, dict):
                yield from get_leaves(v, new_prefix)
            else:
                yield new_prefix

    def compare(exp, act, path):
        """Recursively compare expected and actual at the given path."""
        for key in exp:
            new_path = f"{path}.{key}" if path else key
            if key not in act:
                # Missing subtree: report all leaf paths
                for leaf in get_leaves(exp[key], new_path):
                    missing.add(leaf)
            else:
                exp_val = exp[key]
                act_val = act[key]
                if isinstance(exp_val, dict) and isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    compare(exp_val, act_val, new_path)
                elif isinstance(exp_val, str) and isinstance(act_val, str):
                    if exp_val != act_val:
                        mismatch.add(new_path)
                else:
                    # One is dict, the other is string -> type mismatch
                    mismatch.add(new_path)

        for key in act:
            new_path = f"{path}.{key}" if path else key
            if key not in exp:
                # Extra subtree: report all leaf paths
                for leaf in get_leaves(act[key], new_path):
                    extra.add(leaf)

    compare(expected, actual, "")
    return sorted(missing | extra | mismatch)
```

- Missing fields: paths that exist in expected but not in actual.
- Extra fields: paths that exist in actual but not in expected.
- Type mismatches: paths where both exist but the type string differs.

For nested objects, if a whole subtree is missing or extra, we should report the leaf paths (i.e., all leaf paths under that subtree). For example, if expected has "a" as a dict with "b" and "c", but actual doesn't have "a" at all, then we report "a.b" and "a.c" as missing. Similarly for extra.

If a subtree is present but some inner fields are missing, we report those specific leaf paths.

Implement def bounded_batches(items, max_rows). items is an iterable of (partition,row_count). Preserve order and return a list of batches, each a list of items, whose total rows does not exceed max_rows. An item larger than max_rows must be alone. Reject max_rows <= 0. Consume the iterable once. Return Python code only.

Qwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking

100.0% · 2.5s · stop
```python
def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
    if max_rows <= 0:
        raise ValueError("max_rows must be positive")

    batches = []
    current_batch = []
    current_row_count = 0

    for item in items:
        partition, row_count = item

        # If the item itself exceeds max_rows, it must be alone
        if row_count > max_rows:
            # Flush current batch if not empty
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_row_count = 0
            # Add the oversized item as its own batch
            batches.append([item])
        else:
            # Check if adding this item would exceed max_rows
            if current_row_count + row_count > max_rows:
                # Flush current batch
                if current_batch:
                    batches.append(current_batch)
                # Start new batch with this item
                current_batch = [item]
                current_row_count = row_count
            else:
                current_batch.append(item)
                current_row_count += row_count

    # Don't forget the last batch
    if current_batch:
        batches.append(current_batch)

    return batches
```

reasoning
Here's a thinking process that leads to the suggested solution:

1.  **Understand the Goal:** The objective is to write a Python function `bounded_batches(items, max_rows)` that groups an iterable of items into batches.

2.  **Analyze the Input:**
    *   `items`: An iterable.
    *   Each element in `items` is a tuple `(partition, row_count)`.
    *   `max_rows`: An integer representing the maximum capacity of a batch.

3.  **Analyze the Constraints & Rules:**
    *   *Preserve order:* The items must be processed and yielded in the original sequence.
    *   *Return type:* A list of lists (batches).
    *   *Constraint 1 (Capacity):* The sum of `row_count` in a batch must not exceed `max_rows`.
    *   *Constraint 2 (Oversized items):* If a single item's `row_count` > `max_rows`, it must be in a batch by itself. (Wait, let me re-read the prompt carefully: "An item larger than max_rows must be alone." This implies it *cannot* be combined with others, but since it exceeds the limit, it violates the sum constraint unless it's the only one? Actually, usually "larger than max_rows" means it's impossible to fit, but the prompt explicitly says "must be alone". This suggests the batch containing it will have a sum > max_rows, but it's allowed as a special case. Let's look at the wording again: "whose total rows does not exceed max_rows. An item larger than max_rows must be alone." This is slightly contradictory if taken literally as a hard constraint on the sum. However, standard interpretation for this type of problem is: Fit items into bins. If an item doesn't fit, it gets its own bin, even if it exceeds the limit. Or, perhaps it means "If an item > max_rows, it cannot be combined with *other* items, but it still sits in a batch." Let's assume the strict interpretation: Normal batches sum $\le$ max_rows. If an item > max_rows, it forms a batch of size 1, and that batch sum > max_rows is accepted.)
    *   *Constraint 3 (Validation):* Reject `max_rows <= 0`.
    *   *Constraint 4 (Efficiency):* Consume the iterable once (single pass).

4.  **Algorithm Design:**

    *   *Validation:* Check `if max_rows <= 0: raise ValueError(...)`.
    *   *State:* Need a `current_batch` list and a `current_row_sum` integer.
    *   *Iteration:* Loop through `items`.
    *   *Processing an item `(p, r)`:*
        *   Check if the item fits in the current batch.
        *   Condition: `current_row_sum + r <= max_rows`.
        *   *Edge case (Oversized):* If `r > max_rows`, it *cannot* fit in any batch that respects the sum constraint. The prompt says it "must be alone".
            *   Logic: If `r > max_rows`, we should close the current batch (if not empty) and start a new batch containing just this item.
            *   Logic: If `r <= max_rows` but `current_row_sum + r > max_rows`, we must close the current batch and start a new one with this item.
        *   *Refining the logic:*
            1.  If `current_batch` is empty:
                *   Add item. Update sum.
            2.  If `current_batch` is not empty:
                *   Check if adding `r` keeps sum $\le$ `max_rows`.
                *   If yes: Add to `current_batch`, update sum.
                *   If no:
                    *   Save `current_batch` to results.
                    *   Start new `current_batch` with current item.
                    *   Update sum.
    *   *Handling the specific "larger than max_rows" rule:*
        *   If `r > max_rows`, it will never satisfy `current_row_sum + r <= max_rows` (since `current_row_sum` $\ge$ 0).
        *   So, the logic "If it doesn't fit, start new batch" handles this naturally.
        *   *Wait*, if `r > max_rows`, and we start a new batch, the new batch has sum `r`. Is `r` allowed to be > `max_rows`? Yes, per the prompt "An item larger than max_rows must be alone".
        *   What if `r > max_rows` and we are *already* in a batch? We close the current batch, and the new batch starts with `r`.
    *   *Finalization:* After the loop, if `current_batch` is not empty, append it to results.

5.  **Refining the Logic (Step-by-Step Trace):**

    *   `max_rows = 10`
    *   `items = [(A, 5), (B, 6), (C, 2), (D, 12), (E, 3)]`

    *   Start: `results = []`, `current = []`, `current_sum = 0`.
    *   Item (A, 5): `current` empty. Add A. `current=[(A,5)]`, `sum=5`.
    *   Item (B, 6): `sum + 6 = 11 > 10`. Doesn't fit.
        *   Flush `current` to `results`. `results = [[(A,5)]]`.
        *   New `current = [(B,6)]`, `sum = 6`.
    *   Item (C, 2): `sum + 2 = 8 <= 10`. Fits.
        *   Add C. `current=[(B,6), (C,2)]`, `sum=8`.
    *   Item (D, 12): `sum + 12 = 20 > 10`. Doesn't fit. (Also 12 > 10).
        *   Flush `current` to `results`. `results = [[(A,5)], [(B,6), (C,2)]]`.
        *   New `current = [(D,12)]`, `sum = 12`.
    *   Item (E, 3): `sum + 3 = 15 > 10`. Doesn't fit.
        *   Flush `current` to `results`. `results = [[(A,5)], [(B,6), (C,2)], [(D,12)]]`.
        *   New `current = [(E,3)]`, `sum = 3`.
    *   End of items.
    *   Flush `current`. `results = [[(A,5)], [(B,6), (C,2)], [(D,12)], [(E,3)]]`.

    *   *Self-Correction on "Alone":* Does (D, 12) have to be alone? Yes, the logic creates a new batch for it. Does it have to be *strictly* alone? Yes, because the next item (E, 3) couldn't fit with it (12+3 > 10). What if `max_rows` was 20?
        *   Items: `[(D, 12), (E, 3)]`, `max_rows = 20`.
        *   (D, 12) -> `sum=12`.
        *   (E, 3) -> `12+3 = 15 <= 20`. Fits.
        *   Result: `[(D, 12), (E, 3)]`.
        *   *Prompt Check:* "An item larger than max_rows must be alone."
        *   In the case where `max_rows = 20`, item (D, 12) is *not* larger than max_rows. So it can be with others.
        *   In the case where `max_rows = 10`, item (D, 12) *is* larger than max_rows. It must be alone. My logic creates a new batch for it. If the next item was small enough to fit in a fresh batch but not with D? Wait.
        *   If `max_rows = 10`, `items = [(D, 12), (E, 3)]`.
        *   D starts batch. Sum 12.
        *   E tries to join. 12 + 3 > 10. E starts new batch.
        *   Result: `[[D], [E]]`. D is alone. Correct.
        *   What if `items = [(E, 3), (D, 12)]`?
        *   E starts batch. Sum 3.
        *   D tries to join. 3 + 12 = 15 > 10. D starts new batch.
        *   Result: `[[E], [D]]`. D is alone. Correct.

    *   *Is it possible for a large item to be combined?*
        *   Only if `max_rows` is large enough. But if `item.row_count > max_rows`, it can *never* be combined with anything else (since `row_count + anything > max_rows`).
        *   So the constraint "An item larger than max_rows must be alone" is actually a redundant consequence of the capacity constraint, *unless* the capacity constraint is relaxed for that specific item. But since `row_count > max_rows`, `row_count + x > max_rows` is always true for `x >= 0`.
        *   So, strictly speaking, the "must be alone" rule is automatically satisfied by the sum constraint if we assume row counts are non-negative.
        *   *However*, if the prompt implies that an item > max_rows is allowed to exist in a batch (violating the sum constraint) but *only* if it's the only item, my logic holds. If I allowed it to be combined, it would violate the sum constraint anyway. So the logic is safe.

6.  **Code Structure:**

    ```python
    def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
        if max_rows <= 0:
            raise ValueError("max_rows must be positive")

        batches = []
        current_batch = []
        current_rows = 0

        for partition, row_count in items:
            # Check if item fits in current batch
            # Note: If row_count > max_rows, it won't fit if current_rows >= 0.
            # But specifically, if current_batch is not empty, we check fit.
            # If current_batch is empty, we just add it (even if it exceeds max_rows).

            if current_batch and current_rows + row_count > max_rows:
                # Flush current batch
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_rows = 0

            current_batch.append((partition, row_count))
            current_rows += row_count

        # Don't forget the last batch
        if current_batch:
            batches.append(current_batch)

        return batches
    ```

7.  **Edge Case Analysis:**
    *   `items` empty: Loop doesn't run. `current_batch` empty. Returns `[]`. Correct.
    *   `max_rows` 0 or negative: Raises ValueError. Correct.
    *   Item exactly `max_rows`: `current_rows + row_count` == `max_rows`. Fits. Correct.
    *   Item > `max_rows`:
        *   If `current_batch` empty: Adds it. `current_rows` becomes > `max_rows`.
        *   Next item: `current_rows + next_row > max_rows` (since `current_rows` already huge).
        *   Flushes the huge batch. Starts new one.
        *   Result: Huge item is alone. Correct.
    *   Order preservation: Iterating `items` sequentially and appending to lists preserves order. Correct.
    *   Single pass: `for` loop iterates once. Correct.

8.  **Refining the "Alone" Logic (Just to be paranoid):**
    Is there a scenario where `row_count > max_rows` but it gets grouped?
    Only if `current_rows` is negative? No, row counts are counts.
    Only if `max_rows` logic is bypassed?
    My code: `if current_batch and current_rows + row_count > max_rows`.
    If `current_batch` is empty, we skip the check and add.
    So `[(P, 100)]` with `max_rows=10` -> Batch `[(P, 100)]`. Sum 100.
    Next item `[(Q, 5)]`. `current_batch` not empty. `100 + 5 > 10`. Flush. New batch `[(Q, 5)]`.
    Result `[[ (P, 100) ], [ (Q, 5) ]]`.
    The item (P, 100) is alone.
    What if `items` was `[(Q, 5), (P, 100)]`?
    1. Q added. Batch `[(Q, 5)]`, sum 5.
    2. P comes. `5 + 100 > 10`. Flush Q. New batch `[(P, 100)]`, sum 100.
    Result `[[ (Q, 5) ], [ (P, 100) ]]`.
    Item (P, 100) is alone.
    Logic holds.

9.  **Optimization/Pythonic touches:**
    *   Using `collections.deque` isn't necessary since we return a list of lists, but appending to a list is fine.
    *   The check `if current_batch` is slightly redundant if we initialize `current_rows = 0` and check `current_rows + row_count > max_rows`?
        *   If `current_batch` is empty, `current_rows` is 0.
        *   If `row_count <= max_rows`, `0 + row_count <= max_rows` is True. It adds.
        *   If `row_count > max_rows`, `0 + row_count > max_rows` is True. It enters the `if` block.
        *   Inside `if`: Appends empty list? No, `current_batch` is empty. `batches.append([])`. That's bad. We don't want empty batches in the result.
        *   So the check `if current_batch` is necessary to avoid appending empty lists when an oversized item triggers a flush of an empty buffer (though an empty buffer flush is impossible in this logic flow, except maybe if we initialized differently).
        *   Wait, if `current_batch` is empty, `current_rows` is 0.
        *   If we have an oversized item `row_count > max_rows`.
        *   `if current_batch` (False) -> Skip.
        *   Add item. `current_batch` has item. `current_rows` > `max_rows`.
        *   Next item comes. `if current_batch` (True). `current_rows + next > max_rows`.
        *   Flush. `batches.append(current_batch)` (The oversized one).
        *   Reset `current_batch = []`, `current_rows = 0`.
        *   Add next item.
        *   This works perfectly.

10. **Final Code Construction:**

    ```python
    def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
        if max_rows <= 0:
            raise ValueError("max_rows must be greater than 0")

        batches = []
        current_batch = []
        current_rows = 0

        for partition, row_count in items:
            # If the batch is not empty and adding this item exceeds the limit,
            # we must start a new batch.
            if current_batch and current_rows + row_count > max_rows:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_rows = 0

            current_batch.append((partition, row_count))
            current_rows += row_count

        # Append the final batch if it contains items
        if current_batch:
            batches.append(current_batch)

        return batches
    ```

11. **Reviewing Constraints one last time:**
    *   `items` iterable of `(partition, row_count)`: Handled.
    *   Preserve order: Handled.
    *   Return list of batches (list of lists): Handled.
    *   Total rows <= max_rows: Handled (except for the oversized exception).
    *   Item > max_rows alone: Handled (it forces a split before it if batch not empty, and prevents adding to it after).
    *   Reject `max_rows <= 0`: Handled.
    *   Consume once: Handled (single loop).

    *Self-Correction on "Consume once":* The input is an iterable. Iterating `for ... in items` consumes it. If it's a generator, it's consumed. If it's a list, it's iterated. This is standard.

    Item (2, 12) is alone.

```python
def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
    if max_rows <= 0:
        raise ValueError("max_rows must be positive")

    batches = []
    current_batch = []
    current_rows = 0

    for partition, row_count in items:
        item = (partition, row_count)

        # If this single item exceeds max_rows, it must be alone
        if row_count > max_rows:
            # Flush current batch if non-empty
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_rows = 0
            # Add the oversized item as its own batch
            batches.append([item])
        else:
            # Check if adding this item would exceed max_rows
            if current_rows + row_count > max_rows:
                # Start a new batch
                if current_batch:
                    batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = [item]
                current_rows = row_count
            else:
                # Add to current batch
                current_batch.append(item)
                current_rows += row_count

    # Don't forget the last batch
    if current_batch:
        batches.append(current_batch)

    return batches
```

```python
def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
    if max_rows <= 0:
        raise ValueError("max_rows must be positive")

    batches = []
    current_batch = []
    current_total = 0

    for item in items:
        row_count = item[1]

        if row_count > max_rows:
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_total = 0
            batches.append([item])
        elif current_total + row_count > max_rows:
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_total = 0
            current_batch.append(item)
            current_total = row_count
        else:
            current_batch.append(item)
            current_total += row_count
```

reasoning
Let me think through this problem carefully.

We need to implement `bounded_batches(items, max_rows)` where:
- `items` is an iterable of `(partition, row_count)` tuples
- We need to preserve order
- Return a list of batches, each batch is a list of items
- The total rows in each batch must not exceed `max_rows`
- An item larger than `max_rows` must be alone in its batch
- Reject `max_rows <= 0` (raise an error)
- Consume the iterable once (so we need to convert to a list or iterate through it once)

Let me think about the algorithm:
1. Validate `max_rows > 0`, raise ValueError if not
2. Iterate through items, accumulating them into the current batch
3. For each item, check if adding it would exceed `max_rows`
   - If the item's row_count > max_rows, it must be alone → flush current batch, start new batch with just this item
   - If current batch total + item's row_count > max_rows, flush current batch, start new batch with this item
   - Otherwise, add to current batch
4. At the end, flush the remaining batch

Let me think about edge cases:
- Empty iterable → return empty list
- Item with row_count == max_rows → fits exactly, can be in a batch by itself or with zero-row items
- Item with row_count > max_rows → must be alone (its own batch, even though it exceeds max_rows)
- Items with row_count == 0 → can always be added to any batch

Let me code this up:

```python
def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
    if max_rows <= 0:
        raise ValueError("max_rows must be positive")

    batches = []
    current_batch = []
    current_total = 0

    for item in items:
        row_count = item[1]

        if row_count > max_rows:
            # Item larger than max_rows must be alone
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_total = 0
            batches.append([item])

            # Would exceed max_rows, flush current batch
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_total = 0
            current_batch.append(item)
            current_total = row_count
        else:
            current_batch.append(item)
            current_total += row_count

        batches.append(current_batch)
```

Let me also consider: what if current_batch is empty and we have a normal item? Then current_total is 0, so 0 + row_count <= max_rows (since row_count <= max_rows in that branch), so we just add it. That's fine.

What about the case where current_batch is empty and we're in the `elif` branch? That can't happen because if current_batch is empty, current_total is 0, and 0 + row_count > max_rows would mean row_count > max_rows, which is handled by the first branch.

Actually wait - what if row_count == max_rows exactly? Then 0 + max_rows > max_rows is False, so it goes to the else branch and gets added. Good.

What if we have a batch with total = max_rows - 5, and next item has row_count = 5? Then (max_rows - 5) + 5 = max_rows, which is not > max_rows, so it gets added. Good.

```python
def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
```

- Order is preserved.
- Each batch's total row_count <= max_rows.
- If an item has row_count > max_rows, it must be alone in its batch.
- max_rows must be > 0, else raise an error (reject).
- Consume the iterable once.

def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
    if max_rows <= 0:
        raise ValueError("max_rows must be positive")

    batches = []
    current_batch = []
    current_total = 0

    for item in items:
        _, row_count = item

        if row_count > max_rows:
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_total = 0
            batches.append([item])
            continue

        if current_batch and current_total + row_count > max_rows:
            batches.append(current_batch)
            current_batch = []
            current_total = 0

        current_batch.append(item)
        current_total += row_count

    if current_batch:
        batches.append(current_batch)

    return batches

```python
def merge_payload_ranges(items):
    """
    Merge overlapping or touching ranges only when payloads are equal.

    Args:
        items: List of tuples (start, end, payload)

    Returns:
        List of lists [start, end, payload] representing merged ranges

    Raises:
        ValueError if any item has start > end
    """
    # Validate input and reject start > end
    for start, end, payload in items:
        if start > end:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid range: start ({start}) > end ({end})")

    if not items:
        return []

    # Create a copy to avoid mutating input
    # Sort by start, then by end, then by payload
    sorted_items = sorted(items, key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1], x[2]))

    merged = []

    for start, end, payload in sorted_items:
        if not merged:
            merged.append([start, end, payload])
        else:
            last = merged[-1]
            last_start, last_end, last_payload = last

            # Check if current range overlaps or touches with the last merged range
            # Touching means current start <= last end + 1?
            # Actually, "touching" typically means adjacent: current start == last end + 1 for integer ranges
            # But more generally, overlapping or touching means current start <= last end
            # Wait, let's think about this carefully.
            # Two ranges [a,b] and [c,d] overlap if c <= b and a <= d.
            # They touch if they are adjacent, e.g., [1,2] and [3,4] don't touch in continuous sense but might in discrete.
            # The problem says "overlapping or touching". Let's interpret as: current start <= last end + 1 for integer coordinates,
            # or more precisely, the ranges share a boundary point. For continuous ranges, [1,2] and [2,3] touch at point 2.
            # So condition is: current start <= last end (for overlap) or current start == last end (for touching at endpoint).
            # Actually, standard interpretation: two intervals [a,b] and [c,d] with a<=b, c<=d overlap if max(a,c) <= min(b,d).
            # They touch if one ends where the other begins: b == c or d == a.
            # Combined: they can be merged if current start <= last end (overlap) or current start == last end + something?
            # Let me reconsider: "overlapping or touching" usually means the union is connected.
            # For real numbers: [a,b] and [c,d] have connected union iff c <= b (assuming a<=c after sorting by start).
            # Because if c <= b, then [a,b] U [c,d] = [a, max(b,d)] which is connected.
            # If c > b, there's a gap between b and c.

            # So the condition for merging is: current start <= last end

            if start <= last_end:
                # Ranges overlap or touch
                if payload == last_payload:
                    # Merge them
                    new_end = max(last_end, end)
                    last[1] = new_end
                else:
                    # Different payloads, cannot merge
                    merged.append([start, end, payload])
            else:
                # No overlap or touching, add as new range
                merged.append([start, end, payload])

    return merged
```

```python
def merge_payload_ranges(items):
    """
    Merge overlapping or touching ranges only when payloads are equal.

    Args:
        items: list of (start, end, payload) tuples

    Returns:
        List of [start, end, payload] lists representing merged ranges.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If any item has start > end.
    """
    # Validate input
    for item in items:
        if len(item) != 3:
            raise ValueError("Each item must be a tuple of (start, end, payload)")
        start, end, payload = item
        if start > end:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid range: start ({start}) > end ({end})")

    # Do not mutate input; work with a copy
    # Sort by start, then by end
    sorted_items = sorted([(s, e, p) for s, e, p in items], key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))

    if not sorted_items:
        return []

    # Group by payload first? No, we need to sort by start/end and merge overlapping/touching
    # But merging only happens when payloads are equal.
    # So we can process each payload group separately, or handle it in one pass.
    # Better approach: sort all items, then iterate and maintain a stack/list of current merged ranges.
    # When we encounter a new item, check if it overlaps/touches with existing merged ranges that have the same payload.

    # Actually, let's think about this more carefully.
    # Two ranges can be merged if:
    # 1. They have the same payload
    # 2. They overlap or touch (i.e., next.start <= current.end + 1... wait, "touching" means end >= next.start - 1? Or end >= next.start?)
    # "Touching" typically means the end of one equals the start of another, so they are adjacent.
    # Overlapping means they share some points.
    # So two ranges [a,b] and [c,d] can be merged if c <= b + 1 (assuming integer ranges) or c <= b (if continuous).
    # Let's assume touching means c <= b + 1 for discrete, but more generally c <= b + epsilon.
    # For simplicity, let's say they can be merged if next_start <= current_end + 1 (for integers) or next_start <= current_end (for reals).
    # The problem says "overlapping or touching", which I'll interpret as next_start <= current_end + 1 for integer-like data, but to be safe, let's use next_start <= current_end + 1.
    # Actually, "touching" usually means they meet at a point, so if one ends at 5 and another starts at 5, they touch. So condition is next_start <= current_end + 1? No, if one ends at 5 and another starts at 6, do they touch? Typically no. Touching means they share an endpoint, so next_start == current_end. Overlapping means next_start < current_end.
    # So combined: next_start <= current_end + 1 might be too loose. Let's use next_start <= current_end + 1 only if we consider integer steps.
    # Let me re-read: "Merge overlapping or touching ranges". I'll interpret touching as the end of one being equal to the start of the other. So the condition for merging is next_start <= current_end + 1? No, just next_start <= current_end + 1 seems off.
    # Standard interval merging: intervals [a,b] and [c,d] can be merged if c <= b + 1 (for inclusive integer intervals where [a,b] includes both a and b). But if we're dealing with real numbers, touching would mean c == b.
    # Let's assume the ranges are such that [start, end] is inclusive. Two ranges [a,b] and [c,d] overlap if max(a,c) <= min(b,d). They touch if b == c or d == a.
    # Combined condition for merging: c <= b + 1 (if integer) or c <= b (if real). To be general, let's use c <= b + 1 for safety with integers, but actually the most common interpretation is c <= b + 1 for discrete, c <= b for continuous.
    # I'll go with: next_start <= current_end + 1 to cover both cases reasonably. Actually, let me just use next_start <= current_end + 1.

    # Let me reconsider. A cleaner approach:
    # 1. Sort by start, then end.
    # 2. Use a list to hold merged results.
    # 3. For each item, check against the last few merged ranges (or all) with the same payload.

    # Since merging is transitive within the same payload, we can group by payload and merge within each group.

    from collections import defaultdict

    groups = defaultdict(list)
    for s, e, p in sorted_items:
        groups[p].append((s, e))

    result = []
    for payload, ranges in groups.items():
        # Sort ranges by start, then end (already sorted overall, but let's ensure)
        ranges.sort(key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))

        if not ranges:
            continue

        current_start, current_end = ranges[0]

        for i in range(1, len(ranges)):
            s, e = ranges[i]
            # Check if this range overlaps or touches the current merged range
            # Touching: s <= current_end + 1 (for integer-like) or s <= current_end
            # I'll use s <= current_end + 1 to be safe
            if s <= current_end + 1:
                current_end = max(current_end, e)
            else:
                result.append([current_start, current_end, payload])
                current_start, current_end = s, e

        result.append([current_start, current_end, payload])

    # Sort final result by start, then end
    result.sort(key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))

    return result
```

```python
def merge_payload_ranges(items):
    for start, end, payload in items:
        if start > end:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid range: start {start} > end {end}")

    groups = {}
    for start, end, payload in items:
        groups.setdefault(payload, []).append((start, end))

    result = []
    for payload, intervals in groups.items():
        intervals.sort()
        merged = []
        for start, end in intervals:
            if merged and start <= merged[-1][1]:
                merged[-1] = (merged[-1][0], max(merged[-1][1], end))
            else:
                merged.append((start, end))
        for start, end in merged:
            result.append([start, end, payload])

    result.sort(key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))
    return result
```

```python
def merge_payload_ranges(items):
    # Validate
    for start, end, payload in items:
        if start > end:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid range: start {start} > end {end}")

    # Sort by start, then by end
    sorted_items = sorted(items, key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))

    # Merge
    result = []
    for item in sorted_items:
        start, end, payload = item
        if result and payload == result[-1][2] and start <= result[-1][1]:
            # Merge: extend the end
            result[-1][1] = max(result[-1][1], end)
        else:
            result.append([start, end, payload])

    return result
```

```python
def merge_payload_ranges(items):
    # Validate
    for start, end, payload in items:
        if start > end:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid range: start {start} > end {end}")

    # Group by payload
    from collections import defaultdict
    groups = defaultdict(list)
    for start, end, payload in items:
        groups[payload].append((start, end))

    # Merge within each group
    result = []
    for payload, intervals in groups.items():
        intervals.sort()
        merged = []
        for start, end in intervals:
            if merged and start <= merged[-1][1]:
                merged[-1] = (merged[-1][0], max(merged[-1][1], end))
            else:
                merged.append((start, end))
        for start, end in merged:
            result.append([start, end, payload])

    # Sort final result by start, then end
    result.sort(key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))
    return result
```

This is O(n log n) and handles all cases correctly.

```python
def merge_payload_ranges(items):
    # Validate input and create a copy
    validated = []
    for item in items:
        start, end, payload = item
        if start > end:
            raise ValueError("start must be <= end")
        validated.append([start, end, payload])

    if not validated:
        return []

    # Sort by start, then end
    validated.sort(key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))

    result = []
    for start, end, payload in validated:
        if not result:
            result.append([start, end, payload])
            continue

        last_start, last_end, last_payload = result[-1]
        # Check if we can merge: same payload and intervals overlap or touch
        if payload == last_payload and start <= last_end + 1:
            # Merge: update end to max
            result[-1][1] = max(last_end, end)
        else:
            result.append([start, end, payload])

    return result
```
