Qwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking

100.0% · 2.6s · stop
```python
def committable_windows(events, watermark):
    """
    Process events and return a dict mapping window to list of values.

    Args:
        events: iterable of dicts with keys 'window', 'event_time', 'value'
        watermark: threshold for dropping late events

    Returns:
        dict mapping window to list of values (in input order), with windows
        appearing in first-seen order. Late events (event_time <= watermark) are dropped.
    """
    result = {}
    # Track insertion order using a list of unique windows
    window_order = []

    for event in events:
        event_time = event['event_time']
        # Drop late events: event_time <= watermark is late
        if event_time <= watermark:
            continue

        window = event['window']
        value = event['value']

        if window not in result:
            result[window] = []
            window_order.append(window)

        result[window].append(value)

    # Build ordered dict preserving first-seen order
    from collections import OrderedDict
    ordered_result = OrderedDict()
    for window in window_order:
        ordered_result[window] = result[window]

    return ordered_result
```

```python
def committable_windows(events, watermark):
```

```python
        if event['event_time'] <= watermark:
```

reasoning
Let me break down the requirements:

1. `def committable_windows(events, watermark)`
2. `events` is an iterable of dicts with keys `window`, `event_time`, `value`
3. Drop late events where `event_time <= watermark`
4. For remaining events, return a dict mapping `window` to values in input order
5. Window keys should be inserted in first-seen order
6. Consume the iterable once (so I can't iterate multiple times)
7. Do not mutate `events`

Let me think about the return value:
- It's a dict mapping `window` to values in input order
- So for each window, the values are collected in the order they appear in the input
- The dict itself should have keys in first-seen order (Python 3.7+ dicts maintain insertion order, so this is natural)

So the structure would be: `{window: [value1, value2, ...], ...}` where the list of values is in input order, and the window keys are in the order they were first encountered.

Let me write this:

```python
def committable_windows(events, watermark):
    result = {}
    for event in events:
        if event['event_time'] <= watermark:
            continue
        window = event['window']
        if window not in result:
            result[window] = []
        result[window].append(event['value'])
    return result
```

This:
- Consumes the iterable once (single for loop)
- Does not mutate `events` (just reads from it)
- Drops late events (`event_time <= watermark`)
- Returns a dict mapping window to list of values in input order
- Window keys are inserted in first-seen order (Python 3.7+ dict behavior)

This looks correct and clean.

reasoning
Let me analyze the synthetic evidence:

- EV1: GPU memory was insufficient, so 9.5 GiB of layers were moved to CPU (this is a CPU offload mechanism)
- EV2: Container RSS (Resident Set Size) rose from 7 GiB to 61 GiB (a 54 GiB increase, consistent with the offloaded layers plus overhead)
- EV3: Kernel invoked OOM killer (the system ran out of RAM)
- EV4: GPU had another process using 18 GiB (explaining why GPU memory was insufficient)

Root cause analysis:
- The chain of events: Another process was using 18 GiB on GPU (EV4) → GPU memory was insufficient (EV1) → 9.5 GiB layers were offloaded to CPU (EV1) → Container memory ballooned from 7 to 61 GiB (EV2) → OOM killer was invoked (EV3)
- The root cause is `gpu_to_cpu_offload` — the offloading mechanism triggered a memory cascade that led to OOM.

Action codes (choose exactly two highest priority):
- `disable_cpu_offload` — This directly addresses the root cause. If we disable the offload, the system would fail fast on GPU OOM rather than cascading into CPU RAM exhaustion.
- `vram_guard` — This would prevent the situation by monitoring GPU memory and handling the other process (EV4) or preventing the offload when GPU is occupied by other processes.
- `container_memory_limit` — This would contain the damage but not prevent it.
- `increase_swap` — This is a band-aid.

The two highest priority actions:
1. `disable_cpu_offload` — directly addresses the root cause mechanism
2. `vram_guard` — prevents the triggering condition (GPU occupied by other process) from causing the offload

These two together prevent both the root cause and the triggering condition.

```json
{"root_cause":"optimistic_commit_conflict","action_codes":["refresh_snapshot","bounded_commit_retry"],"explanation":"Both writers read snapshot 812 (EV1); Writer A committed first advancing to 813 (EV2); Writer B's commit was rejected with a ValidationException due to file conflicts against the now-stale base (EV3); retry on a refreshed snapshot succeeded (EV4), confirming a classic optimistic concurrency conflict resolved by snapshot refresh and bounded retry."}
```
reasoning
Let me analyze the synthetic evidence:

- EV1: Two writers read snapshot 812
- EV2: Writer A commits snapshot 813
- EV3: Writer B fails with ValidationException: conflicting files
- EV4: Retry on refreshed snapshot succeeds

This is a classic optimistic concurrency control scenario:
1. Both writers read the same base snapshot (812)
2. Writer A commits first, advancing to snapshot 813
3. Writer B's commit fails because the base snapshot is stale — the files it tried to write conflict with what Writer A already committed
4. Writer B retries with a refreshed snapshot and succeeds

Root cause: `optimistic_commit_conflict` — This is exactly what happened. Two writers based their work on the same snapshot, one committed first, the other's commit was rejected due to the conflict.

Action codes (exactly two, highest priority):
- `refresh_snapshot` — This is what actually resolved the issue (EV4 shows retry on refreshed snapshot succeeds). This is the primary action.
- `bounded_commit_retry` — The retry mechanism is what allowed recovery. Without a bounded retry, the refresh alone wouldn't help.

The other options:
- `idempotent_write` — Not relevant here; the issue isn't about duplicate writes
- `delete_metadata` — Not relevant; no metadata corruption

So the two highest-priority actions are `refresh_snapshot` and `bounded_commit_retry`.

EV3 writer B fails with ValidationException due to conflicting files, indicating a classic optimistic concurrency conflict. EV4 confirms that retrying after refreshing the snapshot resolves the issue, so the root cause is optimistic commit conflict, with retry and snapshot refresh as the highest-priority actions."}
reasoning
我们根据证据：EV1两个写入者读取快照812，EV2写入者A提交快照813，EV3写入者B失败ValidationException: conflicting files，EV4重试刷新快照后成功。这是典型的乐观并发冲突，因为两个写入者基于同一快照，一个先提交，另一个冲突。根因是optimistic_commit_conflict。行动代码：bounded_commit_retry（重试）和refresh_snapshot（刷新快照）是合适的。idempotent_write不适用，delete_metadata不适用。所以答案。

   - **Evidence 3 (EV3):** Executors have free memory and no GC pause. This rules out memory issues or GC overhead. So `executor_memory_shortage` is unlikely.
   - **Evidence 4 (EV4):** Stage waits for one task. This is a classic symptom of stragglers caused by skew. The stage is blocked waiting for the slowest task (the one handling the skewed partition/hot key).
   - **Root Cause Options:** `shuffle_key_skew`, `executor_memory_shortage`, `scheduler_outage`
   - **Action Codes Options (choose exactly two highest-priority):** `salt_hot_key`, `adaptive_skew_join`, `repartition_by_distribution`, `increase_all_memory`

reasoning
Let me analyze the synthetic evidence provided:

- EV1: median task duration = 20s, max = 1080s. This is a huge disparity — the median is 20s but the max is 1080s (18x the median). This strongly suggests a few tasks are taking much longer than others, which is a classic sign of data skew.

- EV2: one partition has 62% of rows for key UNKNOWN. This directly confirms key skew — a single key (UNKNOWN) is responsible for the majority of the data in one partition. This is the root cause of the long-running task.

- EV3: executors have free memory and no GC pause. This rules out executor_memory_shortage as the root cause.

- EV4: stage waits for one task. This confirms that the bottleneck is a single task (the one processing the skewed partition), consistent with the skew diagnosis.

Root cause: `shuffle_key_skew` — clearly supported by EV2 (62% of rows for one key) and EV1 (huge duration disparity).

Now for action codes, I need to choose exactly two from:
- `salt_hot_key` — salting the hot key (UNKNOWN) to distribute it across multiple partitions. Directly addresses the root cause.
- `adaptive_skew_join` — adaptive handling of skew during joins. Also directly addresses the root cause.
- `repartition_by_distribution` — repartitioning based on data distribution. Less targeted than the first two.
- `increase_all_memory` — irrelevant since EV3 shows memory is fine.

The two highest-priority actions are `salt_hot_key` and `adaptive_skew_join`. These are the most targeted solutions for a known hot key causing skew.
